420. JAMES CUNNINGHAM (21) , Unlawfully uttering counterfeit coin.
MR. MEAD Prosecuted; MR. COLE Defended.
ELIZA CROCKER . My husband keeps the Rose and Crown beershop, Rodney Road, Walworth—on Saturday night, 26th February, about 10 minutes to 12, the prisoner and four or five others were in front of the bar—he paid me 1 s. for some cigars—I put it in the till, and gave him a 6 d. and three or four pence change—it was rather bright—a minute or two afterwards he called for a pot of ale, which came to 4 d., and paid with a bright 6 d., which I put in the till—he then asked for two or three cigars, and gave me 1 s., bright like the other; I put it in the till, and gave him the change—my husband then called "Time," and they all went outside, but the prisoner rushed in again for half an ounce of tobacco, and put 1 s. on the counter—I said "Have not you coppers, I have given you change before?"—he said "No"—I bent the 1 s. with my teeth, and said "This is a nice thing"—I am not sure whether he picked it up or whether my husband gave it to him—he left without the tobacco—I then looked in the till and found two bad shillings and a bad 6 d., new-looking, similar to those I took from the prisoner; about 3 s. 6 d. in worn coins was also in the till—I picked the prisoner out from 20 or 30 others at the station.
Cross-examined. There had been a friendly lead upstairs, and the potman waited on the people there; they assembled about 8.30, and left at 12 o'clock—they came down and drank in the bar before leaving—my husband called in a man from outside and asked him if he knew of the counterfeit coin, but I said, "That is not the man"—my husband said that he should want 2 s., and another young man gave it to him.
Re-examined. The young man was in front of the bar while the prisoner was there, but I cannot say whether he had any of the beer—they all went out together.
HENRY CHARLES CROCKER . I keep the Rose and Crown—on 26th February, before I closed the house, a man, who I have no doubt is the prisoner, though I will not swear it, asked for some tobacco, and put down a 1 s.—my wife said, "Have not you any coppers? you have given me several pieces of silver"—I either threw it on the counter or put it in his hand, and told him to take his book —he left directly, and my wilt went to
the till and gave me two bad shillings and a bad sixpence—I went out immediately and brought back a man whom I had seen upstairs that evening, and charged him—he said that it was not him—I said, "I shall want half a crown, otherwise I shall lock you up"—he gave me nothing, but a friend of his, named Blackgrove, gave me 2 s. and then I let the man go—next day, Sunday, between 1 and 3 p.m., the prisoner came and had some drink—I did not know him—he said, "I have been accused of passing bad money; Dick Blackgrove has been round to me this morning and told me what occurred last night, and that he had to pay 2 s. to get the man out, and it is arranged that we are to meet here to-day"—I said that I did not know anything about it; all I wanted was the 2 s. from Blackgrove and 6 d. for myself—he said, "I shan't pay it"—he then left—he did not give me any money; Blackgrove did not come—on 9th March I was called to the police-station, and saw the prisoner there—I gave the coins to the constable; these are them ( produced)
Cross-examined. I do not know the name of the man I charged—the prisoner denied passing the coin.
LEONABD WABD . On 28th February I saw a description of the prisoner—I saw him at 1 o'clock next morning in the Walworth Road—I went behind him and said, "Cunningham"—he said, "Halloa; what is it?" I said, "You are wanted for uttering counterfeit coin on the 26th of last month"—he said, "Well, I am d—d"—I took him him to the station and found on him twopence, a Hanoverian medal, and a knife—Mr. Crocker pointed him out from 20 or 30 other prisoners at the police-court, and gave me these two shillings and this sixpence.
Cross-examined. The medal has a big hole through it.
WILLIAM WEBSTER . These two shillings and this sixpence are bad—these medals are made for whist markers.
The Prisoner, in his Statement before the Magistrate, said that he gave Mrs. Crocker the same sixpence back which she had given to him in change for the first shilling, and that the second shilling was put down by a man who offered him a cigar, and that he did not know that the third shilling was bad
ELIZA CROCKER ( Re-examined) I did not give the prisoner a sixpence which he had given me, nor did he give me one which I had given him—he gave me a bright one, and the one I gave him was not bright.
The Prisoner received a good character
GUILTY of the last uttering — Six Months' Imprisonment
[trialAccount t18810328-421]
421. GEORGE GREEN (30) and ALFRED KING (29) , Unlawfully uttering counterfeit coin.
MR. POLAND Prosecuted
MARIA PERKINS . I am barmaid at the Princess of Wales, Borough Road—on 9th March, about 4.50 p.m., the prisoners came in, and I served one with some stout and the other with some whisky—one of them put down this coin ( produced)—I saw that it was not a genuine half-sovereign, and threw it on the counter and said, "Do you take me for a fool?"—King said, "It is a good half-sovereign," tried it between his teeth, and put it on the counter—Green said, "It is a good one; I took it at the Elephant and Castle"—the manager took it up and a constable was sent for—Green then took out a shilling and said, "I want the half-sovereign back"—they were given in custody.
JOHN FISHER . I am manager at the Princess of Wales—I saw the prisoners and received this coin—I sent for a constable—Green said, "I took it for a bet at the Elephant and Castle," and offered to pay with a shilling—he said that it was a good half-sovereign, and wanted me to give it back to him, which I refused—the Elephant and Castle is about 100 yards off.
WILLIAM NASH ( Policeman J 216). I was called and took King—I found a halfpenny on him—on the way to the station he said, "I don't know anything about the other man; I met him, and he asked me to have something to drink"—I heard Green say at the station, "Me and the other prisoner went into the public-house to get change to divide a bet which we had received from a man at the Elephant and Castle."
JOHN GALLIJER . ( Policeman L 199). On 9th March I went to the Princess of Wales, and found the prisoners there—I searched Green and found on him a good shilling and a halfpenny—nothing was found on King—I took Green to the station; he said, "I took the money from a betting-man at the Elephant and Castle, and we both went into the Princess for King to have a share of it."
SOPHIA SIMMONS . I am barmaid at the Two Eagles—I served the prisoners on this afternoon, after 3 o'clock, and Green gave me a coin exactly like this—I examined it and said, "This is not a half-sovereign"—he said, "Oh, it is all right; it is one of the old Georges"—I showed it to my mistress—I came back and told Green that I could not take it—he threw down sixpence and I gave him the change—one of them said that they took it on the racecourse—our house is about five minutes' walk from the Princess of Wales.
Cross-examined by Green. I swear to you, but I did not pick you out because I was nervous—the policeman did not tap me on my shoulder.
Cross-examined by King I do not swear to you now; you stood sideways all the time you were in the bar—I did not swear to you in the first instance.
WILLIAM WEBSTER . This is a genuine 6 d. gilt, and of the reign of George III.—it is quite new, and when tried with the teeth is quite hard—it has not the word "sixpence" on it.
Green's Statement before the Magistrate "What Simmons says is false; the other gentleman speaks truth."
Green's Defence. We took the half-sovereign between us at the Elephant and Castle for a bet, and went there to share the money.
GUILTY . GREEN then PLEADED GUILTY to a previous conviction of an assault with intent to rob. GREEN.— Fifteen Months' Imprisonment. KING.— Nine Months' Imprisonment
Before Mr. Recorder
[trialAccount t18810328-422]
422. EDWARD LEPINE (62) and ROBERT JONES (30) , Burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling house of Gilson Reeve, and stealing rings, watches, bracelets, and other articles, his property.
MR. LEVEY Prosecuted; MR. FRITH Defended
WILLIAM JONES ( Police Inspector V) On the morning of 5th March my attention was called to the prosecutor's house—an entry had been effected from the first-floor front room into the shop by moving some boards, and dropping down the lift into the shop—the till had been forced, and a quantity of jewellery stolen—I received information on the
12th, and went to Mr. Jones's shop, 81, Gravel Lane, Soutllwark, four or five miles from the prosecutor's place—I saw both the prisoners there—I told Lepine I was a police inspector, and had come to see him respecting a watch which had been seen in his possession on the previous Saturday—he hesitated a moment, and then said, "I have not got it; 1 have sold it for 7 s. 6 d."—Mr. Reeve came in at that time—I said, "That watch is part of the proceeds, of a burglary"—I asked Jones to show me several watches which were in the window, and he produced 14 of the stolen watches, and one which I got afterwards made 15—Mr. Reeve identified them as they were shown to him—I asked Jones how he came in possession of them—he said, "I bought them on Tuesday or Wednesday last as a job lot over the counter of a tall man who I do not know—I gave him 6 s. each for the yellow ones and 8 s. each for the white ones"—I asked if he had any entry of the transaction in his book; he said, "I keep no books whatever"—I searched his premises, but found no other property connected with the robbery—I only found some very old account books—we took the prisoners into custody.
Cross-examined. I understood Lepine to be Jones's servant—he keeps a small, dirty shop—one or two watches were in the window, and six were in the shop—there was no concealment—the police serve all jewellers with a notice of stolen property, and I have a notice describing some of these as silver watches, a copy of which was served on Jones on the very morning that he alleges he bought them—they are described as silver, and their numbers are here—the notice is dated March 8th; it gives the actual numbers engraved on each watch.
Re-examined The notice does not say whose watches they are; it says, "Any person offering the undermentioned articles for sale or in pledge will be given into custody"—the name of "Jones" is over the shop.
GILSON REEVE . I am a pawnbroker, of 8, Falcon Market, Battersea—on Saturday morning, 5th March, I found my shop broken into—I missed a large quantity of property, amongst which were some watches—I identify these watches produced by my initial scratched on the cases, and the numbers of them correspond with the numbers in this police notice—I gave information—there are silver ones among them, valued from 8 s. to 15 s., and aluminium, about 6 s. each—I went with the last witness to Jones's house; he said he bought the watches of a strange tall man.
Cross-examined. The most expensive watch is 15 s.; it is real silver—I found six silver ones on Jones.
STEPHEN BANNINGTON . I am a whitesmith, of 135, Trafalgar Street, Walworth—on 12th March I bought these watches of Lepine, at my house, for 7 s. 6 d.—he had done a little job for my daughter—he brought a watch I had given him to clean, and said, "I have got a very nice watch to suit you; it is a good one, a good movement; I gave 6 s. for it"—he did not say to whom—he said he took it to a pawnbroker on the Saturday night, who offered him 5 s. for it.
Cross-examined. He said it was Mr. Reeve's, and I knew him, there was no concealment—I have known him upwards of ten years, and know that he has lived in Walworth Road thirty years—I always believed him to be a respectable man—I knew him latterly as Jones's servant—I am not a customer of Jones's—I was never inside his shop.
JAMES O'DJUC ( Police Sergeant V). On 14th March I went with
Inspector Jones to the prisoner Jones's house—he gave the inspector these 14 watches—I put Lepine into the cab, and said, "You understand what you are taken in custody for?" he said, "No"—I said, "You are going to be charged with Jones with committing a burglary at 8, Falcon Road, Battersea, on the night of the 5th;" he said, "I did not go to work on the Tuesday, and when I went on Wednesday Jones told me had bought some watches from a man over the counter on Tuesday, I know nothing about the robbery"—Jones was not present.
Cross-examined. Lepine was at work in the shop—some watches were lying on a glass shelf, and others were taken out of the cases, and the weres were under a glass, they were undergoing repair.
JAMES HENRY GIBBS . ( not examined in chief).
Cross-examined. I am Mr. Reeve's manager—I know Lepine as a working watch repairer for Jones—I understood him to be Jones's servant—he actually brought one of these watches to our shop after my master had been robbed of it, and asked me to look at it—I did so—he asked me what I would lend him on it—I said, "5 s."—he said, "I bought it this morning, and I should lose 4 s. by it"—I gave it back to him, and he went away—our shop is about 100 yards from one of the prosecutor's.
Re-examined. The shop where the burglary was committed is at Clapham, four miles away—the name of Gibson Reeve is up at the shop in Union Street—I cannot say whether it is also at Battersea—I identify the watch as one of the stolen watches—I told Mr. Reeve, and was directed to give it back to Lepine, saying nothing.
GIBSON REEVE . ( Re-examined). I also have a shop at Clapham Junction, Battersea—my name is up there, "Gilson Reeve"—nothing is said about business being carried on anywhere else—my other shop is in the Borough—it there says "And at Whitecross Street"—I lost a great many other things, and have put a mark against them on the printed list, also a great many things which are not in the list—there were five or six dozen rings; none of them were found at the prisoner's.
EMILY FROST . I am housekeeper to the prosecutor at 8, Falcon Market—on the night in question I went round the house about 11.45—it was quite safe—I went to bed at 11.50—I was disturbed about 3.15 by noises like the chopping of wood and a bell ringing, and there were various noises—I sat up in bed—when I went down at 7.15 I found the street door and the windows open, which had been closed over night and the place in the great confusion—some one had got in through the top staircase window, which was wide open—they could get there from the back; there was a scaffold there; that window was closed when I went to bed—they could get down the staircase, and then get through the lift leading to the shop.
LAWRANCE LEWIS . I am in Mr. Reeves's employ at his fourth shop, 96, Park Street, Southwark—on 14th March I went to Jones's shop to buy a watch—he shewed me one—I bought it, and asked him to show me another, pointing it out—I gave the one I bought to Mr. Reeve; it is one of those produced—I was instructed to go and buy a watch—he did not show me the second one; he assigned no reason for not doing so.
Cross-examined. There were two other watches like it lying in the window—I know he is a watch-cleaner.
Jones received a good character.
NOT GUILTY .

424. FREDERICK LANE (22), was indicted for wilful and corrupt perjury, upon which MR. MORICE, for the Prosecution, offered no evidence
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18810328-425]
425. THOMAS KING (26) , Unlawfully uttering counterfeit coin.
MR. MEAD Prosecuted
HENRY BATE . I am a chemist, of 44, Thorn Road, South Lambeth—on 15th March, about 3.80, I served the prisoner with a seidlitz-powder—he gave me 1 s.—I put it into the till, where there were no other shillings, and gave him the change—in about half an hour Hubbard came with Mr. Read, and from what they said I looked in the till, found the shilling, and gave it to Hubbard—no one else had served in the shop since the prisoner came—this is the seidlitz-powder; the label has been torn off, but sufficient remains for me to identify it.
Cross-examined by the Prisoner. I did not positively say at the station taat you were the man, but I had no doubt whatever—I also said at the police-court that I believed you were the man, and I say so now; I am not doubtful.
CHARLES REED . I am am a baker of South Lambeth Road—on 15th March, about 4.30, my wife served the prisoner with two halfpenny biscuits—he put down 1 s.—she gave him the change—I looked at the coin before he left, and saw that it was bad—I went after him, stopped him, and told him he had given me a bad shilling—he said, "I was not aware of it. Some man gave it to me to come and get some biscuits"—I said, "You had better come back to the shop with me"—he called to some man who was walking across to the other side of the road, but he took no notice—I took the prisoner back to the shop, and while waiting for a constable he took the seidlitz-powder out of his pocket, and began to tear off the label—I gave him in charge with the coin.
Cross-examined. I did not see a man running away—you offered me all the money yon had, and said that you would call and pay the rest.
SAMUEL HUBBARD . ( Policeman W 310). I was fetched to Mr. Read's shop, and he gave the prisoner in custody for giving him a bad shilling—I searched him, and found on him sixpence in silver, three halfpence, and this seidlitz-powder—I saw the name of Bate on the label, and I went there with Mr. Read—I produce the two shillings I received from Mr. Read and Mr. Bate—I asked the prisoner his address—he refused to give any.
WILLIAM WEBSTER . These coins are both bad—not from the same mould.
The prisoner in his defence stated that he received the coins and seidlitz-powder from another man.
GUILTY — Nine Months' Imprisonment.
[trialAccount t18810328-426]
426. PETER SMART (23) was indicted for a like offence.
MR. MEAD Prosecuted.
CHARLES THOMPSON . I keep the Phoenix public-house, Stacey Street, Soho; on 17th February, about 7.40 p.m., I served the prisoner with a pint of four ale, which came to twopence; he tendered a half-crown,
which I noticed was base at once—I said, "Are you aware this is bad, Charley?"—he said, "I was not aware of it, sir; I never did a thing like it Wore; if you like you can take off my boots and stockings and search me"—I said, "How did you come by it?"—he said, "I think I got it at Billingsgate Market this morning"—I detained him and sent for a constable, and gave the half-crown to him—the prisoner was charged next day at Marlborough Street, remanded, and then discharged, there being no further evidence against him—this is the half-crown—I had not known him before, though—I called him Charlie.
DANIEL REDMOND ( Policeman E 159). I was called to the Phoenix, and received the prisoner in custody with this half-crown.
GEORGE BURTON ( Policeman L 81). On the evening of 1st March, about 9.15, I was with Constable Baker in Newington Butts, and saw the prisoner with another man—they were shortly joined by a third, and they went towards Kennington Park—they stopped outside Mr. Fossi's ice-shop—I lost sight of the prisoner there—the other two went across the road and stood against the park railings in the dark—they all three joined again, and the prisoner went into Mr. Wilson's, came out, and went into Church Street, the other two following him—I sent Baker in to Mr. Wilson's—when he came out they saw us and ran off—I caught the prisoner and said, "I shall take you in charge for uttering a counterfeit florin"—he said, "I was just coming from Clapham when two men asked me if my name was Peter Smart, and if I would have a pint of beer; they gave me the money; I did not know it was bad; I did not know the men; they sent me in"—he afterwards said, "I only knew them by sight"—I afterwards received a florin from Mr. Fossi.
GEORGE BAKER ( Policeman L 244). I was with the last witness—I went into the tobacconist's shop and spoke to Mr. Wilson—I saw the prisoners and the two others standing under a lamp in Church Street—I afterwards made a search there and found the bad florin and a penny.
FARINO FOSSI . I keep an ice shop at 244, Kennington Park Road—about 9.30 on the evening of 18th March a young man, who I could not swear to, paid me this florin—I gave him 1 s. 9 d. change—about an hour after the police came, and I looked in the till and found that the florin was bad—there was no other florin there—I gave it to the constable.
THOMAS WILSON . I am manager to Mrs. Moxon, of 328, Kennington Park Road, tobacconist—on 1st March, about 9.45, the prisoner came for half an ounce of tobacco and tendered a florin—I told him it was bad—he wanted me to hand it back to him, which I did after breaking it in the detector—he said he was not aware it was bad, and tendered a penny, which he said was all the money he had besides the florin—very soon after Baker came in.
WILLIAM WEBSTER . This half-crown is bad; the two florins are also bad, and from the same mould.
GUILTY .— Nine Month' Imprisonment.
Before Mr. Justice Hawkins.
[trialAccount t18810328-427]
427. DAVID THOMAS DAVIES (21) , Feloniously wounding Catherine Davies, with intent to murder her. Second Count, with intent to do grievous bodily harm.
MESSRS. POLAND and MONTAGU WILLIAMS Prosecuted; MR. LILLEY Defended.
CATHERINE DAVIES . I am the prisoner's wife, and live at 55, Gravel Lane, Southwark—we keep a general shop—on the 13th December, a little after twelve at night, my husband was putting up the shutters—there were some boxes in the way—he asked me to remove them, which I refused to do—he did nothing to me; it was my fault entirely—he moved the boxes himself, and came to me in a passion, and I flew at him and struck him in the face with my fist—he did not strike me back—we were scuffling together, and both of us fell by the door leading to the shop, and I felt that I was stabbed, but how I can't say—I did not see any knife—I said I was stabbed—he said I was not, for he never touched the knife—we were by the door between the shop and the parlour, at the top of the steps—there was a large knife kept on a slab just by the door for cutting the bacon—I was wounded in the stomach, on the left side—the knife penetrated all my clothes; I was dressed as usual—I suppose a doctor was sent for; I hardly remember anything after that—the doctor attended me at home, I dare say over two months, but not so frequently lately—I was told that I was likely to die, and I made a statement, as well as I could, in the presence of my husband and the inspector.
Cross-examined. We had been married three years, and lived very comfortably together—my husband has never struck me; he was a good husband and good to my child—he had been out on this night and might have had a little drink—I was in a great passion and hardly knew what I did; I believe I did say "You have been out whoremongering again"—in the scuffle our clothes might have touched the slab on which the knife was, it projects a little over the steps—in the scuffle I tore the lappel of his coat and scratched his face—I might have scratched his wrist with my nails.
By the COURT. He was not in the habit of drinking—we had been the best of friends during the morning; there had been nothing to put me out, only I thought he was rather bad tempered, and I thought I would be the same—I don't know why I used that offensive expression to him—I was in such a dreadful rage and bad temper I hardly knew what I said.
HANNAH BEVINS . I am unmarried, and now live at 85, Chatham Street, Walworth; I formerly lived at 55, Gravel Lane—on the night of the 13th December about 12.20 I saw the prisoner putting up the shutters—I went away and returned in about 10 minutes; I then found the shop closed—I heard Mrs. Davies scream "help and murder" and heard some one fall—I unlocked the door and went in, and saw her lying on the ground in the passage near the parlour door; the prisoner was leaning over her—she said "I am stabbed"—I asked him if he had done it; he said "No"—she was lifted into the parlour into a chair by Mr. Fotherby—I didn't see any knife about; there were no supper things on the table—they occupied the shop and two parlours at the back.
Cross-examined. When I first went in the prisoner seemed to be assisting her—I had lodged there about three months; during that time they seemed to live comfortably together.
MARY ANN FOTHERBY . I am the wife of Charles Daniel Fotherby—on 13th December I was living at 55, Gravel Lane; we occupied the first floor front room—I have known the prisoner and his wife some time; he generally went out to work in the daytime and Mrs. Daviea attended to
the shop—about 12.30 on this night I was in bed; I heard the shop closed and the shutters being put up—about five or six minutes after that I heard Mrs. Davies scream out help and murder—my husband got up and ran downstairs; he called me, and I went down and found Mrs. Davies in the arm-chair in the parlour and the prisoner standing by her—she said "Undress me; let the gentlemen go out of the room and I will show you"—they went out and I pulled her clothes aside, and saw that she was stabbed on the left side of the lower part of the stomach—I said to the prisoner, "Mr. Davies, have you stabbed her?" he said "No"—she said "You liar, you have"—my husband went for the doctor—the prisoner tried to get some brandy for his wife, and I went upstairs to get a sofa for her—I saw no knife anywhere—the furniture in the parlour was all straight except one chair; that was right over in a corner of the room.
Cross-examined. They were in the house about two months while I was there, as far as I know they lived comfortably together—I heard some scuffling before I went down.
CHARLES DANIEL FOTHERBY . I live at 55, Gravel Lane—on 13th December about 12.20 I heard a little bit of scuffling downstairs in the parlour underneath—I got out of bed and listened, and after two or three minutes I heard "murder and help" screamed—I ran downstairs and saw Mrs. Davies lying in the passage, and the prisoner close to her—I pulled her up and put her on a chair, she said she was stabbed—I said, "Where?" she said, "Here, but I can't tell you"—I said, "If you are stabbed, where is the knife?"—I looked about but could not see one—the prisoner said he had done nothing—I went for the doctor.
Cross-examined. I have known the prisoner some time—as far as I know he has always behaved in a proper manner—I have not spoken to him above three or four times—he was very drunk on this night, and did not know what he was doing; he was like mad with drink, and when he saw what he had done he turned all manner of colours.
CHARLES GRAHAM ( Police Inspector M). On the morning of the 14th a little after 2 I went to 55, Gravel Lane—I saw the prisoner in the passage—I asked if he was the landlord, he said, "Yes"—we went into the back parlour—I found Mrs. Davies there, and Dr. Wilson and his assistant—I asked Mrs. Davies who had committed the assault upon her, she replied, "My husband"—he made no reply at that time—in consequence of what the doctor said I thought it right to take a statement from her—the prisoner was present—it was made in answer to questions that I and Dr. Wilson put to her—I took it down and read it over to her, and she put her mark to it: this is it. ( Read: "I believe I am in a dying state, and make the following statement voluntarily. My husband and I have not for the last two days been on good terms. He has been drinking. He struck me first with his fist. I defended myself as well as I could. He had then no knife in his hand when he was putting up the shutters. He found some wooden boxes in his way. He got angry at this, and struck me with the shop knife. When I received the blow I rushed to the door and screamed 'Murder.' Mrs. Fotherby came. I showed the wound to her. I am positive my husband done it; there was no other person in the room. I don't know the cause of the quarrel. I was perfectly sober. I had had a portion of a quartern of gin between four of us, one of whom was my husband's mother.") When she had made
this statement I sent the prisoner to the station, and then searched the house—I found these two knives ( produced)—this long thin one was on the counter in the shop, about eight feet from the parlour door—this broad one was on the bacon-slab beside the door, a few inches from it—the thin knife was clean, the other had a stain on the point of it—I could not see what the stain was, it was dry—I called Dr. Wilson's attention to it—I hare the clothes which the prosecutrix was wearing—the doctor and I compared the holes in these clothes with the broad knife; the hole went through the apron, dress, and petticoats—the knives were shown to Mrs. Davies, who said she could not identify either of them as the knife with which she was stabbed—I read over the charge to the prisoner at the station; he wanted to make a statement—I cautioned him, and said I would take it down; it might be used against him in evidence—he said, "Very well"—he then made this statement. ( Read: "As soon as I. came, home my wife accused me of being out whoremongering again, which I denied, and then started putting up the shutters. There were some cheese-boxes in my way, which annoyed me. I told her to move the things out of the way, when she said, 'B—them; move them yourself'. I then made a rush at her, and attempted to strike her. We went into the parlour and had a free fight. In the scuffle she took up the knife and drawded it across my wrist, which was marked longways. She got the knife from the bacon-board in the shop. In the struggle she got her stab.") I read it over to him and he signed it—I looked at his wrist, and noticed that there was a mark on it—I believe it was the left wrist—it appeared to be a mark from finger-nails, a scratch—it was fresh, but not bleeding then—I called Dr. Wilson's attention to it next day—the prisoner also had some scratches on his right cheek, apparently made with a finger-nail; they were fresh.
Cross-examined. I found the knife on the bacon slab—there are two steps leading from the parlour to the shop—I found the prosecutrix in the sleeping-room; the prisoner was about speaking while we were questioning her, and he was stopped by Dr. Wilson—he said "Hush"—he thought the woman was dying—this dress is a sort of black stuff, the petticoat of flannel; there were no stays—the wound on the prisoner's wrist was about six inches long, extending from the wrist up the arm—I found some dinner knives in a drawer in the parlour; none of those were pointed.
JAMES VANCE YOUNG . I am assistant to Dr. Wilson—I was called by Fotherby to see Mrs. Davies—I arrived about 1.10—I found a wound about four inches above the left groin, with the bowels protruding; I sent for Dr. Wilson.
HENRY WILSON , M.R.C.S. I live at 38, Great Charlotte Street—on the morning of 14th December, shortly after I o'clock, I went to Mrs. Davies—I found her suffering from a punctured wound low down on the left side of the abdomen, the abdominal wall was perforated, and the intestines protruded about a foot and a half; one portion of the intestine was divided in about half its circumference—we removed her to her bed in the adjoining room—I had to enlarge the wound in order to return the intestine, and I then sewed up the external wound; the operation took some considerable time—afterwards, while the woman's statement was being made, the prisoner kept interrupting—I told him for his own sake not to speak—the long knife was brought to me; the woman said
she did not know that it was done with that knife; in my opinion it was probably done with this broad knife—the woman was in great danger at the time—I have attended her ever since; every day for about six weeks, and at intervals since—I think she has quite recovered now—I have seen the clothes ( produced)—the holes in them exactly correspond with the larger knife—the clothes would scarcely offer any resistance—I examined the prisoner's wrist the following day—I could not positively say how the wound was caused; probably by a scratch; it might have been from the point of a knife.
Cross-examined. He was very excited—he was not in a fit state to speak—he appeared to be excited by drink—I told him he should speak afterwards if he wished to do so—he kept contradicting each statement that his wife made, and as she was supposed to be in a dying state it caused great confusion—there was only one wound—I did not pay any attention to the knives till the next day—there were some rusty red marks upon it, and some bacon grease—I did not examine it under the microscope.
HENRY HEANEY . ( Police Sergeant M 17). I took the prisoner into custody—his coat was torn down at the lappel—he said, "This is the way she treats me; look at my coat"—I saw two scratches on his right cheek—I did not notice the mark on his wrist—he said nothing about it.
The prisoner received a good character.
GUILTY of Unlawfully Wounding — Fifteen Months' Imprisonment.
ADJOURNED TO MONDAY, MAY 2 ND, 1881.
